<a id='question-127'></a>

### Question 127: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 3 Focus 27 (of Effector)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) and natural killer (NK) cells in eliminating infected or cancerous cells, specifically focusing on the recognition mechanisms involving MHC class I molecules and the ligands recognized by inhibitory receptors on NK cells. (理解细胞毒性T淋巴细胞(CTLs)和自然杀伤(NK)细胞在清除受感染或癌变细胞中的作用，特别是关注涉及MHC I类分子以及NK细胞抑制性受体识别配体的识别机制。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 55-year-old male with a history of chronic lymphocytic leukemia (CLL) undergoes an allogeneic hematopoietic stem cell transplant (HSCT) from an HLA-matched unrelated donor. The conditioning regimen includes fludarabine and cyclophosphamide. Post-transplant, he develops fever, severe watery diarrhea, and a maculopapular rash. Skin biopsy reveals apoptotic keratinocytes. Flow cytometry of peripheral blood lymphocytes shows a significant decrease in CD8+ T cells and an increase in CD56+ CD16+ cells. Further investigation reveals that the donor cells express normal levels of MHC class I molecules, but the recipient's residual T cells and the donor CD8+ T cells show impaired cytotoxic activity against target cells expressing a specific viral antigen. The patient is subsequently diagnosed with acute graft-versus-host disease (GVHD) and cytomegalovirus (CMV) infection. The impaired cytotoxic activity is most likely due to a deficiency in which of the following?

(A) Perforin and granzyme B secretion by CD8+ T cells
(B) Expression of Fas ligand (FasL) on CD8+ T cells
(C) Recognition of MHC class I molecules by T cell receptors (TCRs)
(D) Expression of ligands for inhibitory receptors (e.g., MICA/B, MICB) on target cells
(E) Upregulation of PD-1 on CD8+ T cells

#### Correct Answer: (A)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient History:** 55-year-old male with CLL undergoing allogeneic HSCT. CLL is associated with T-cell dysfunction and immunosuppression. HSCT involves conditioning that further suppresses the immune system.
- **Post-Transplant Complications:** Fever, watery diarrhea, maculopapular rash are classic signs of acute GVHD. Skin biopsy showing apoptotic keratinocytes is characteristic of GVHD.
- **Immunological Findings:** Decreased CD8+ T cells (likely due to conditioning regimen and CLL) and increased CD56+ CD16+ cells (NK cells, which can mediate GVHD).
- **Key Finding:** Impaired cytotoxic activity of donor CD8+ T cells against viral antigen-expressing target cells, despite normal MHC class I expression on donor cells. This suggests a defect in the effector mechanism of CTLs.
- **Diagnosis:** Acute GVHD and CMV infection. GVHD is mediated by donor T cells attacking recipient tissues. CMV infection is common post-HSCT due to immunosuppression. The question focuses on the mechanism of impaired CTL function.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the most likely cause of the impaired cytotoxic activity.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Cell-Mediated Cytotoxicity:** CTLs (CD8+ T cells) and NK cells are key effector cells.
- **CTL Activation & Killing:** CTLs recognize viral antigens presented by MHC class I molecules on infected cells via their TCR. Upon activation, CTLs release cytotoxic granules containing perforin and granzymes. Perforin forms pores in the target cell membrane, allowing granzymes (serine proteases) to enter and induce apoptosis. CTLs can also induce apoptosis via the Fas/FasL pathway.
- **NK Cell Activation & Killing:** NK cells recognize target cells lacking MHC class I (often seen in viral infections or tumors) or expressing stress-induced ligands (e.g., MICA/B, MICB) for activating receptors. NK cells also have inhibitory receptors (e.g., KIRs) that recognize MHC class I. If inhibitory signals outweigh activating signals, the NK cell is inhibited. NK cells kill target cells similarly to CTLs, using perforin and granzymes.
- **Impaired Cytotoxicity in Vignette:** The vignette describes impaired cytotoxic activity of CD8+ T cells (CTLs) against target cells, despite normal MHC class I expression. This points towards a defect in the effector mechanism itself, specifically the release of cytotoxic molecules.
- **Option (A) Perforin and granzyme B secretion:** This is the primary mechanism by which CTLs and NK cells induce apoptosis in target cells. A deficiency in this pathway would directly lead to impaired cytotoxicity. This aligns with the clinical finding.
- **Option (B) Expression of Fas ligand (FasL):** FasL is another mechanism for CTL-mediated killing, but perforin/granzyme is considered the major pathway. While FasL deficiency could impair killing, perforin/granzyme deficiency is a more common and direct cause of severely impaired cytotoxicity.
- **Option (C) Recognition of MHC class I molecules by TCRs:** The vignette states that donor cells express normal MHC class I. Impaired TCR recognition would typically occur if MHC class I was downregulated or if the TCR itself was defective, neither of which is suggested.
- **Option (D) Expression of ligands for inhibitory receptors (e.g., MICA/B, MICB):** These ligands are typically *activating* signals for NK cells, promoting killing. Increased expression might lead to enhanced NK cell killing, not impaired CTL killing. Decreased expression might lead to NK cell inhibition. This is irrelevant to the impaired CTL function described.
- **Option (E) Upregulation of PD-1:** PD-1 is an inhibitory receptor expressed on T cells. Upregulation of PD-1 (e.g., by chronic antigen exposure or tumor cells) leads to T cell exhaustion and impaired function. While possible in chronic conditions, the vignette describes acute post-HSCT impairment, and the primary mechanism of CTL killing is the release of perforin/granzymes. PD-1 primarily affects T cell activation and proliferation, though it can also impact effector function. However, a direct defect in the effector molecules (perforin/granzymes) is a more direct explanation for the observed impaired cytotoxicity.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): **Incorrect.** This is the correct answer. Impaired secretion of perforin and granzyme B directly inhibits the cytotoxic function of CD8+ T cells and NK cells, leading to the observed phenotype.
- (B): **Incorrect.** FasL is involved in CTL-mediated apoptosis, but perforin/granzyme is the predominant mechanism. A deficiency in FasL would impair killing, but it's less likely to be the primary cause of such profound impairment compared to a defect in the major cytotoxic pathway.
- (C): **Incorrect.** The vignette explicitly states that MHC class I expression is normal on donor cells. Impaired TCR recognition would require a defect in the TCR itself or the antigen presentation machinery, which is not indicated.
- (D): **Incorrect.** MICA/B and MICB are ligands for activating receptors on NK cells. Their expression typically promotes NK cell killing. A deficiency in their expression might inhibit NK cells, but it doesn't explain impaired CTL function.
- (E): **Incorrect.** PD-1 upregulation leads to T cell exhaustion and reduced effector function. While this can occur, especially in chronic viral infections or cancer, the vignette describes an acute post-transplant setting where a primary defect in the cytotoxic machinery (perforin/granzyme) is a more direct explanation for the observed impaired killing. Furthermore, the question asks for the *most likely* deficiency causing the *impaired cytotoxic activity*, and a defect in the effector molecules themselves is the most direct cause.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | CTL Killing Mechanism (Correct) | NK Cell Killing Mechanism | Fas/FasL Pathway | PD-1 Pathway |
|-------------------------|---------------------------------|---------------------------|-------------------|--------------|
| **Primary Effector Molecules** | Perforin & Granzymes          | Perforin & Granzymes      | FasL (on CTL)     | N/A          |
| **Target Recognition**    | TCR-MHC I-Peptide              | Inhibitory/Activating Receptors | Fas (on target)   | N/A          |
| **Role in Vignette**      | Impaired secretion leads to impaired cytotoxicity | N/A                     | Secondary pathway | T cell exhaustion |
| **Relevance to Impaired Cytotoxicity** | Direct cause                 | N/A                     | Less common cause | Can cause impairment |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cell-mediated cytotoxicity by CD8+ T cells and NK cells relies primarily on the release of perforin and granzymes to induce apoptosis in target cells. Defects in this pathway lead to impaired killing. Differentiate this mechanism from other pathways like Fas/FasL, recognition mechanisms (TCR/MHC, NK cell receptors), and inhibitory pathways (PD-1). Remember that NK cells are crucial in controlling viral infections and tumors, especially when MHC I expression is reduced.

---

<a id='question-128'></a>

### Question 128: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 3 Focus 28 (Infection)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific T cell subsets (Th1, Th2, Th17, Treg, CTLs) and their associated cytokines in controlling different types of infections, particularly intracellular pathogens, and recognizing the clinical consequences of deficiencies in these pathways. (理解特定T细胞亚群（Th1、Th2、Th17、Treg、CTL）及其相关细胞因子在控制不同类型感染中的作用，特别是胞内病原体，并认识到这些途径缺陷的临床后果。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-week history of worsening cough, fever, night sweats, and significant weight loss. He is HIV-positive, with a CD4+ T cell count of 85 cells/µL, and is not currently on antiretroviral therapy. Chest X-ray reveals bilateral diffuse interstitial infiltrates. Sputum analysis shows acid-fast bacilli (AFB) on Ziehl-Neelsen staining. Culture of the sputum grows *Mycobacterium avium* complex (MAC). Despite initiation of clarithromycin and ethambutol, the patient's symptoms continue to worsen, and he develops pancytopenia. Bone marrow biopsy reveals numerous granulomas containing AFB-positive macrophages. Which of the following immunological mechanisms is MOST deficient in this patient, contributing to his disseminated MAC infection and poor response to therapy?

(A) Impaired production of IgE by B cells leading to reduced eosinophil activation.
(B) Defective activation of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) leading to inadequate killing of infected cells.
(C) Reduced secretion of IL-12 by dendritic cells and macrophages, impairing Th1 differentiation.
(D) Decreased production of IL-4 by Th2 cells, resulting in insufficient B cell class switching to IgG1.
(E) Insufficient generation of regulatory T cells (Tregs), leading to excessive inflammation and tissue damage.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, HIV-positive, CD4+ count 85 cells/µL (severe immunosuppression), not on ART. This immediately points towards opportunistic infections common in advanced AIDS.
*   **Symptoms:** Chronic cough, fever, night sweats, weight loss (constitutional symptoms suggestive of chronic infection, often tuberculosis or atypical mycobacterial infections).
*   **Imaging:** Bilateral diffuse interstitial infiltrates (consistent with pulmonary infection, including *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PJP) or disseminated mycobacterial infection).
*   **Microbiology:** Sputum AFB positive (suggests mycobacteria), culture grows *Mycobacterium avium* complex (MAC). MAC infection is a common opportunistic infection in advanced AIDS (CD4 < 50).
*   **Clinical Course:** Worsening symptoms despite clarithromycin and ethambutol (standard MAC therapy). Development of pancytopenia and granulomas in bone marrow (indicating disseminated disease).
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST deficient immunological mechanism contributing to disseminated MAC and poor treatment response.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **MAC Pathogenesis:** MAC is an intracellular pathogen, primarily residing within macrophages. Effective control requires cell-mediated immunity, specifically the Th1 response.
*   **Th1 Response:** Macrophages infected with MAC present antigens to T cells. Dendritic cells and macrophages produce IL-12 in response to MAC components (like lipoarabinomannan). IL-12 is crucial for driving the differentiation of naive CD4+ T cells into Th1 cells.
*   **Th1 Function:** Th1 cells produce IFN-γ. IFN-γ activates macrophages, enhancing their ability to kill intracellular MAC via reactive oxygen species (ROS) and nitric oxide (NO). IFN-γ also promotes the development of CTLs, which can kill infected cells.
*   **HIV and Th1 Deficiency:** HIV directly infects and depletes CD4+ T cells, including Th1 cells. Furthermore, HIV infection can impair the function of dendritic cells and macrophages, leading to reduced IL-12 production. This results in a profound deficiency in the Th1 response, which is critical for controlling intracellular pathogens like MAC.
*   **Clinical Correlation:** The patient's severe immunosuppression (CD4 < 50) directly correlates with the inability to mount an effective Th1 response. The disseminated MAC infection (granulomas in bone marrow, pancytopenia) and poor response to therapy are classic manifestations of this immunodeficiency. Standard MAC therapy relies on macrophage activation (mediated by IFN-γ), which is impaired in Th1 deficiency.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) Impaired production of IgE by B cells leading to reduced eosinophil activation.** IgE and eosinophils are primarily involved in defense against helminthic parasites and allergic reactions (Th2 response). While HIV can affect B cell function, the primary defect in controlling MAC is cell-mediated immunity (Th1), not humoral immunity related to IgE. This is incorrect.
*   **(B) Defective activation of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) leading to inadequate killing of infected cells.** While CTLs (CD8+ T cells) play a role in controlling intracellular pathogens, the primary defect in disseminated MAC infection in AIDS is the failure of macrophage activation mediated by Th1 cells and IFN-γ. CTLs are less critical for controlling MAC within macrophages compared to the Th1/IFN-γ axis. Although HIV affects CTL function, the most prominent deficiency relevant to MAC control is the Th1 defect. This is less likely than (C).
*   **(D) Decreased production of IL-4 by Th2 cells, resulting in insufficient B cell class switching to IgG1.** IL-4 is the signature cytokine of Th2 cells, promoting B cell class switching to IgE and IgG1, and involved in defense against extracellular parasites. A deficiency in Th2 function would impair these processes. MAC is an intracellular pathogen controlled by Th1, not Th2. This is incorrect.
*   **(E) Insufficient generation of regulatory T cells (Tregs), leading to excessive inflammation and tissue damage.** Tregs (often characterized by CD4+CD25+FoxP3+) suppress immune responses and maintain tolerance. While immune dysregulation occurs in HIV, the primary problem in controlling MAC is the lack of *effector* immunity (Th1), not excessive suppression by Tregs. In fact, Treg numbers can sometimes be increased in HIV, but their dysfunction is complex and not the primary reason for susceptibility to MAC. This is incorrect.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Disseminated MAC in AIDS (Correct Answer: Th1 Deficiency) | PJP in AIDS (Th1/Th17) | Toxoplasmosis in AIDS (Th1) | Helminthic Infection (Th2) |
| :------------------ | :------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------- | :------------------------ | :----------------------- |
| **Primary Immune Defect** | Impaired Th1 response (IL-12, IFN-γ)                   | Impaired Th1/Th17    | Impaired Th1              | Impaired Th2             |
| **Key Cytokines**   | IL-12, IFN-γ deficient                                   | IL-17, IFN-γ deficient | IFN-γ deficient           | IL-4, IL-5, IL-13 deficient |
| **Pathogen Type**   | Intracellular bacteria (macrophages)                     | Fungus (Pneumocystis) | Protozoan (Toxoplasma)    | Helminth (worm)          |
| **Cell Type**       | Macrophages, Th1 cells                                   | Macrophages, T cells  | Macrophages, T cells      | Eosinophils, B cells    |
| **Clinical Manifestations** | Disseminated granulomas, pancytopenia                 | Diffuse pneumonitis  | Encephalitis, chorioretinitis | Eosinophilia, allergy    |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The control of intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium avium* complex (MAC) relies heavily on cell-mediated immunity, specifically the Th1 response driven by IL-12 and IFN-γ, which activates macrophages. Severe CD4+ T cell depletion in advanced HIV infection leads to a profound Th1 deficiency, predisposing patients to disseminated MAC infection. Key differentials for opportunistic infections in AIDS include *Pneumocystis jirovecii* pneumonia (PJP), toxoplasmosis, and cytomegalovirus (CMV), each requiring different aspects of cell-mediated immunity for control. Understanding the specific T cell subset and cytokine pathways involved in controlling different pathogens is crucial.

---

<a id='question-129'></a>

### Question 129: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 3 Focus 29 (Proliferation)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of IL-2 and its receptor (IL-2R) in T cell proliferation and activation, particularly in the context of graft rejection and immunosuppression. (理解 IL-2 及其受体 (IL-2R) 在 T 细胞增殖和激活中的作用，特别是在移植物排斥和免疫抑制的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 52-year-old male with a history of chronic kidney disease secondary to hypertension receives a kidney transplant from a deceased donor. He is started on standard immunosuppressive therapy, including cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, and prednisone. Three months post-transplant, he develops acute onset fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, and elevated serum creatinine. A renal biopsy reveals acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates. Flow cytometry analysis of the peripheral blood shows a significant decrease in CD8+ T cells and an increase in CD25 expression on remaining T cells. Serum IL-2 levels are markedly reduced. Which of the following mechanisms is most likely responsible for this patient's acute kidney transplant rejection?

(A) Antibody-mediated complement activation leading to endothelial injury.
(B) Impaired T cell proliferation due to cyclosporine-induced inhibition of calcineurin.
(C) Direct viral cytopathic effect on renal tubular epithelial cells.
(D) IL-2 receptor deficiency preventing T cell activation and proliferation.
(E) Macrophage-mediated inflammation due to bacterial translocation from the gut.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 52-year-old male, kidney transplant recipient, history of chronic kidney disease. This sets the stage for potential transplant rejection.
- **Immunosuppression:** Standard triple therapy (cyclosporine, mycophenolate mofetil, prednisone) is used to prevent rejection.
- **Clinical Presentation:** Acute onset fever, flank pain, decreased urine output, elevated creatinine 3 months post-transplant. These are classic signs and symptoms of acute rejection.
- **Renal Biopsy:** Acute tubular necrosis and interstitial inflammation with lymphocytic infiltrates confirm acute cellular rejection.
- **Flow Cytometry:** Decreased CD8+ T cells and increased CD25 expression on remaining T cells. CD25 is the alpha chain of the IL-2 receptor (IL-2Rα). Increased expression indicates T cell activation, but the decrease in CD8+ cells suggests ongoing rejection despite immunosuppression.
- **Serum IL-2 Levels:** Markedly reduced. IL-2 is a crucial cytokine for T cell proliferation and clonal expansion.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the mechanism responsible for the acute rejection.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Acute Cellular Rejection:** This is a T cell-mediated process where recipient T cells recognize donor MHC molecules as foreign.
- **T Cell Activation & Proliferation:** T cell activation requires two signals: TCR engagement with MHC-peptide complex (Signal 1) and co-stimulatory signals (Signal 2, e.g., CD28-B7). Following activation, T cells upregulate IL-2 and its receptor (CD25, the IL-2Rα chain). IL-2 binding to IL-2R triggers intracellular signaling cascades (JAK-STAT pathway) leading to T cell proliferation, differentiation, and effector functions (e.g., cytotoxicity by CD8+ T cells).
- **Cyclosporine Mechanism:** Cyclosporine is a calcineurin inhibitor. Calcineurin is a phosphatase required for the activation of NFAT (Nuclear Factor of Activated T cells). NFAT translocates to the nucleus and promotes transcription of IL-2 and other cytokine genes. By inhibiting calcineurin, cyclosporine blocks NFAT activation, thereby suppressing IL-2 production. Reduced IL-2 leads to impaired T cell proliferation and clonal expansion, preventing the full development of the immune response against the graft.
- **Clinical Correlation:** The patient is experiencing acute cellular rejection despite being on cyclosporine. The low IL-2 levels and increased CD25 expression (indicating T cell activation attempt) strongly suggest that cyclosporine is inhibiting IL-2 production, which is a key step in T cell proliferation required for rejection. The decreased CD8+ T cells could be due to apoptosis or migration into the graft, but the underlying mechanism preventing full-blown rejection is the immunosuppression. The question asks for the *mechanism* responsible for the rejection *process* itself, which is T cell proliferation, and how it's being affected. Cyclosporine directly inhibits this proliferation pathway.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Antibody-mediated complement activation leads to *humoral* rejection (also known as chronic antibody-mediated rejection or AMR), characterized by donor-specific antibodies (DSAs) binding to donor HLA antigens, activating complement, and causing endothelial damage. This typically presents later and involves different immunological markers (e.g., C4d deposition). The vignette describes acute *cellular* rejection with lymphocytic infiltrates and T cell markers.
- (C): Direct viral cytopathic effect (e.g., CMV infection) can cause graft dysfunction, but it typically involves viral inclusions in tubular cells and wouldn't primarily manifest as T cell-mediated inflammation or be directly linked to IL-2 suppression by immunosuppressants. While viral infections can trigger rejection, the primary mechanism described here points to the immunosuppressant's effect on T cell proliferation.
- (D): IL-2 receptor deficiency (e.g., in severe combined immunodeficiency, SCID) is a primary immunodeficiency, not an acquired condition related to transplant rejection or immunosuppression. Patients with IL-2R deficiency would have profound T cell lymphopenia from birth and would not survive long enough to receive a transplant. The patient has increased CD25 expression, indicating the receptor is present, but its function is impaired downstream due to lack of IL-2.
- (E): Macrophage-mediated inflammation due to bacterial translocation can occur post-transplant, especially with immunosuppression, but it's not the primary mechanism of acute *cellular* rejection characterized by lymphocytic infiltrates and T cell activation markers. This would typically involve signs of systemic bacterial infection and different inflammatory cell profiles.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Acute Cellular Rejection (Correct) | Antibody-Mediated Rejection (A) | Viral Infection (C) | IL-2R Deficiency (D) | Bacterial Translocation (E) |
|-------------------------|------------------------------------|---------------------------------|--------------------|-----------------------|-----------------------------|
| **Primary Cells**       | T lymphocytes (CD8+, CD4+)         | Antibodies, Complement, Endothelium | Infected cells    | T cells (absent/non-functional) | Macrophages, Neutrophils   |
| **Mechanism**           | T cell recognition of donor MHC    | DSA binding, Complement activation | Viral replication | Lack of IL-2 signaling | Bacterial invasion          |
| **Biopsy Findings**     | Lymphocytic infiltrates           | Endothelialitis, C4d deposition | Viral inclusions   | N/A (Primary defect)  | Neutrophilic infiltrates    |
| **Key Cytokine**        | IL-2 (for proliferation)           | N/A                             | Interferons       | N/A                   | TNF-α, IL-1β                 |
| **Immunosuppression Effect** | Blocks T cell proliferation (e.g., Cyclosporine blocks IL-2 production) | May not fully prevent DSA production | May not prevent infection | N/A                       | Increases risk of infection |
| **IL-2 Levels**         | High (during rejection)            | Normal                          | Normal/High       | Normal (but unresponsive) | Normal                      |
| **CD25 Expression**     | Increased (on activated T cells)   | Normal                          | Normal             | Normal                | Normal                      |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Understanding the role of IL-2 and its receptor in T cell proliferation is crucial for understanding cell-mediated immunity and transplant rejection. Cyclosporine inhibits calcineurin, blocking IL-2 gene transcription and thus T cell proliferation. Differentiating acute cellular rejection (T cell mediated) from antibody-mediated rejection (humoral) based on clinical presentation, biopsy findings, and immunological markers is a key USMLE Step 1 concept. Remember that immunosuppressants target specific steps in the immune response, often affecting cytokine production or cell signaling pathways.

<a id='question-130'></a>

### Question 130: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 3 Focus 30 (response)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of different T cell subsets (Th1, Th2, Th17, Treg) and their associated cytokines in orchestrating immune responses, particularly in the context of intracellular pathogens and chronic inflammation. (理解不同 T 细胞亚群（Th1、Th2、Th17、Treg）及其相关细胞因子在协调免疫应答中的作用，特别是在细胞内病原体和慢性炎症的背景下。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-week history of worsening cough, fever, night sweats, and significant weight loss. He is HIV-positive with a CD4 count of 85 cells/µL. He reports no recent travel or known exposure to tuberculosis. Chest X-ray reveals bilateral interstitial infiltrates, predominantly in the upper lobes, with evidence of cavitation. Sputum samples are positive for acid-fast bacilli (AFB) on Ziehl-Neelsen staining. Culture confirms the presence of *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*. Despite initiation of standard quadruple anti-tuberculosis therapy (isoniazid, rifampin, pyrazinamide, ethambutol), the patient's symptoms continue to progress, and new cavitary lesions appear on follow-up imaging after 4 weeks. Further investigation reveals persistently elevated levels of IL-10 and TGF-β in his bronchoalveolar lavage fluid, along with a decreased proportion of IFN-γ producing T cells upon stimulation with *M. tuberculosis* antigens.

Which of the following cellular immune mechanisms is MOST likely impaired in this patient, contributing to the lack of response to standard anti-tuberculosis therapy?

(A) Impaired activation of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) leading to reduced killing of infected macrophages.
(B) Defective differentiation of Th1 cells and subsequent reduced production of IFN-γ.
(C) Excessive production of IL-4 and IL-5 by Th2 cells, promoting eosinophil recruitment and alternative macrophage activation.
(D) Dysfunctional regulatory T cells (Tregs) failing to suppress excessive inflammation, leading to tissue damage.
(E) Reduced production of IL-17 by Th17 cells, impairing neutrophil recruitment and bacterial clearance.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, HIV-positive, severely immunocompromised (CD4 < 100). This immediately points towards opportunistic infections and atypical presentations.
- **Symptoms:** Chronic cough, fever, night sweats, weight loss – classic symptoms of tuberculosis (TB).
- **Radiology:** Bilateral upper lobe infiltrates with cavitation – typical radiographic findings for reactivation TB.
- **Microbiology:** AFB positive sputum, confirmed *M. tuberculosis* culture. Diagnosis of TB is established.
- **Treatment Failure:** Lack of response to standard quadruple anti-TB therapy despite 4 weeks. This suggests an underlying immune defect hindering bacterial clearance.
- **Immunological Findings:** Elevated IL-10 and TGF-β (immunosuppressive cytokines), decreased IFN-γ producing T cells upon *M. tuberculosis* stimulation. These findings strongly implicate a defect in the Th1 response, which is crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens like *M. tuberculosis*.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Tuberculosis Pathogenesis:** *M. tuberculosis* is an intracellular bacterium primarily infecting alveolar macrophages. Effective control requires cell-mediated immunity, specifically a robust Th1 response.
- **Th1 Response:** Activated macrophages present *M. tuberculosis* antigens via MHC class II to CD4+ T cells. In the presence of IL-12 (produced by macrophages and dendritic cells), naive CD4+ T cells differentiate into Th1 cells.
- **Th1 Cytokines:** Th1 cells produce IFN-γ, which is the key cytokine for activating macrophages to kill intracellular *M. tuberculosis* via reactive oxygen species (ROS) and nitric oxide (NO). Th1 cells also produce TNF-α, which promotes granuloma formation and macrophage activation.
- **HIV and TB:** HIV infection, particularly with low CD4 counts, severely impairs Th1 responses. CD4+ T cells are the primary target of HIV, and their depletion directly affects Th1 cell development and function. Furthermore, HIV can directly infect macrophages, impairing their ability to present antigens and produce IL-12. The elevated IL-10 and TGF-β observed in the patient are immunosuppressive cytokines often associated with chronic infections and immune dysregulation, further suppressing the Th1 response.
- **Lack of Response to Therapy:** Standard anti-TB drugs kill extracellular bacteria and bacteria within actively dividing macrophages. However, they are less effective against dormant bacteria within granulomas. Effective clearance of TB requires a strong Th1 response to activate macrophages to kill both actively dividing and dormant bacilli. The impaired Th1 response in this HIV-positive patient explains the lack of clinical and radiographic improvement despite appropriate antibiotic therapy.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Impaired activation of cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs) leading to reduced killing of infected macrophages. While CTLs (CD8+ T cells) can recognize *M. tuberculosis* antigens presented on MHC class I molecules on infected cells (including macrophages), their role in controlling TB is considered secondary to the Th1 response and macrophage activation by IFN-γ. The primary defect in controlling intracellular *M. tuberculosis* is macrophage activation, driven by Th1/IFN-γ, not CTL killing of infected macrophages. The vignette specifically points to impaired IFN-γ production, linking to Th1 dysfunction.
- (C) Excessive production of IL-4 and IL-5 by Th2 cells, promoting eosinophil recruitment and alternative macrophage activation. Th2 responses are typically associated with helminth infections and allergic reactions. IL-4 and IL-5 promote eosinophil recruitment and alternative macrophage activation (M2), which is generally detrimental in controlling intracellular pathogens like *M. tuberculosis*. While immune dysregulation can occur in HIV, the specific findings (elevated IL-10/TGF-β, decreased IFN-γ) strongly point against a dominant Th2 response being the primary cause of treatment failure in TB. Th2 responses are typically suppressed in TB.
- (D) Dysfunctional regulatory T cells (Tregs) failing to suppress excessive inflammation, leading to tissue damage. Tregs (CD4+CD25+FoxP3+) normally suppress excessive immune responses to prevent autoimmunity and tissue damage. While Tregs play a role in TB pathogenesis (potentially limiting the protective Th1 response), the vignette describes *suppressed* Th1 function (decreased IFN-γ) and elevated *immunosuppressive* cytokines (IL-10, TGF-β), which are more consistent with a failure of Th1 activation rather than a failure of Treg suppression leading to excessive inflammation. In fact, Tregs might be *overactive* or the Th1 response is simply too weak to overcome suppression. The core issue highlighted is the lack of IFN-γ.
- (E) Reduced production of IL-17 by Th17 cells, impairing neutrophil recruitment and bacterial clearance. Th17 cells produce IL-17, which promotes neutrophil recruitment and is important for extracellular bacterial and fungal infections. While Th17 cells can contribute to TB immunity, the primary mechanism for controlling intracellular *M. tuberculosis* within macrophages is the Th1/IFN-γ pathway. The vignette's findings (decreased IFN-γ, elevated IL-10/TGF-β) directly implicate Th1 dysfunction, making this option less likely to be the *most* significant factor contributing to treatment failure in this specific context.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature             | Correct Answer (B) - Defective Th1/IFN-γ | (A) Impaired CTLs | (C) Excessive Th2/IL-4 | (D) Dysfunctional Tregs | (E) Reduced Th17/IL-17 |
|----------------------|------------------------------------------|-------------------|------------------------|-------------------------|------------------------|
| **Primary Defect**   | Th1 differentiation/IFN-γ production     | CTL activation    | Th2 polarization      | Treg suppression        | Th17 differentiation   |
| **Key Cytokine**     | IFN-γ (decreased)                        | N/A               | IL-4, IL-5 (increased) | IL-10, TGF-β (increased)| IL-17 (decreased)      |
| **Immune Cell Type** | CD4+ Th1 cells                           | CD8+ CTLs         | CD4+ Th2 cells         | CD4+CD25+ Tregs        | CD4+ Th17 cells        |
| **Target Pathogen**  | Intracellular (e.g., *M. tuberculosis*) | Virus-infected cells | Helminths, Allergens  | Self-antigens, Pathogens| Extracellular bacteria |
| **Macrophage Activation**| M1 (enhanced)                           | N/A               | M2 (alternative)      | Variable               | N/A                   |
| **Clinical Relevance**| Susceptibility to intracellular infections| Viral infections  | Allergy, Helminths     | Autoimmunity, Immunoregulation| Extracellular infections|

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The cornerstone of immunity against intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* is the Th1 response, characterized by IFN-γ production. Severe immunosuppression, such as in advanced HIV infection, leads to impaired Th1 differentiation and function, resulting in uncontrolled infection and poor response to therapy. Key cytokines to remember are IFN-γ (Th1), IL-4/IL-5 (Th2), IL-17 (Th17), and IL-10/TGF-β (immunosuppressive). Differentiate TB pathogenesis from other infections (e.g., helminths requiring Th2, extracellular bacteria requiring Th17).

<a id='question-131'></a>

### Question 131: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 3 Focus 31 (antigen)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific antigen presentation by MHC class II molecules on antigen-presenting cells (APCs) to CD4+ T helper cells, and the subsequent activation of these T cells, which is crucial for orchestrating adaptive immune responses, including B cell activation and cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) activation. (理解MHC II类分子在抗原提呈细胞 (APCs) 上向 CD4+ T 辅助细胞提呈特异性抗原的作用，以及随后 T 细胞的激活，这对于协调适应性免疫反应至关重要，包括 B 细胞激活和细胞毒性 T 淋巴细胞 (CTL) 激活。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the emergency department with a 3-day history of fever, chills, severe headache, and photophobia. He is a known intravenous drug user and has a history of HIV infection, currently not on antiretroviral therapy (ART). Physical examination reveals nuchal rigidity and altered mental status. A lumbar puncture is performed, and cerebrospinal fluid (CSF) analysis shows elevated protein, low glucose, and numerous neutrophils. Gram stain of the CSF reveals Gram-negative diplococci. Empiric treatment with ceftriaxone and vancomycin is initiated. Despite initial improvement, the patient develops worsening neurological symptoms, including seizures and focal deficits, 10 days after starting antibiotics. MRI of the brain shows multiple ring-enhancing lesions. CSF cultures remain negative after 72 hours. A repeat CSF analysis reveals a significant increase in lymphocytes, with a predominance of CD4+ T cells, and a markedly elevated level of interferon-gamma (IFN-γ). Which of the following is the most likely underlying immunological mechanism contributing to the patient's persistent neurological symptoms despite antibiotic therapy?

(A) Formation of immune complexes leading to vasculitis in the brain parenchyma.
(B) Direct cytopathic effect of the pathogen on neurons and glial cells.
(C) Activation of CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes targeting infected brain cells.
(D) Defective phagocytosis of the pathogen by microglia due to complement deficiency.
(E) Aberrant activation of CD4+ T helper cells leading to excessive cytokine production and inflammation.

#### Correct Answer: (E)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, IV drug user, HIV-positive (not on ART). This establishes a high-risk individual for opportunistic infections, particularly those affecting the central nervous system (CNS), and indicates potential immunodeficiency.
- **Initial Presentation:** Fever, chills, headache, photophobia, nuchal rigidity, altered mental status. Classic signs and symptoms of bacterial meningitis.
- **CSF Findings (Initial):** Elevated protein, low glucose, numerous neutrophils, Gram-negative diplococci. Strongly suggestive of bacterial meningitis, likely caused by *Neisseria meningitidis*.
- **Treatment & Course:** Empiric antibiotics (ceftriaxone, vancomycin) initiated. Initial improvement followed by worsening neurological symptoms (seizures, focal deficits) after 10 days. This suggests a complication or a different underlying process beyond simple bacterial infection.
- **Imaging:** MRI shows multiple ring-enhancing lesions. This finding is characteristic of brain abscesses or granulomas, often seen in opportunistic infections or inflammatory conditions.
- **CSF Findings (Repeat):** Increased lymphocytes, predominance of CD4+ T cells, markedly elevated IFN-γ. This indicates a significant cell-mediated immune response within the CNS, specifically involving T helper cells. The elevated IFN-γ is a key cytokine produced by activated T cells, particularly Th1 cells.
- **Cultures:** CSF cultures remain negative after 72 hours. This is common in partially treated bacterial meningitis or in cases where the causative agent is difficult to culture or is non-bacterial.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the *underlying immunological mechanism* contributing to persistent neurological symptoms despite antibiotic therapy. The key is the shift from neutrophilic pleocytosis to lymphocytic pleocytosis dominated by CD4+ T cells and high IFN-γ, occurring *after* initial antibiotic treatment for presumed bacterial meningitis.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **Initial Infection:** The patient likely had bacterial meningitis caused by *Neisseria meningitidis*, supported by the initial CSF findings (Gram-negative diplococci, neutrophilic pleocytosis, low glucose).
- **Antibiotic Treatment:** Ceftriaxone is effective against *N. meningitidis*. Initial improvement suggests the antibiotics were working on the bacterial infection.
- **Paradoxical Worsening:** The worsening neurological symptoms, MRI findings (ring-enhancing lesions), and CSF shift (lymphocytic pleocytosis, CD4+ T cell predominance, high IFN-γ) point towards an immune-mediated process occurring *after* the initial bacterial insult and antibiotic treatment.
- **Immune Response:** The high levels of CD4+ T cells and IFN-γ indicate a strong Th1-type response. IFN-γ is a potent activator of macrophages and is crucial for cell-mediated immunity against intracellular pathogens. In this context, it's likely driving an inflammatory response within the brain.
- **Mechanism:** The initial bacterial infection (likely *N. meningitidis*) causes inflammation and tissue damage. The antibiotics reduce the bacterial load but may not completely eradicate it, or the damage triggers a persistent immune response. The patient's HIV infection (not on ART) likely impairs their ability to regulate this immune response effectively. The CD4+ T cells, recognizing bacterial antigens (or possibly self-antigens released due to tissue damage), become activated and release large amounts of pro-inflammatory cytokines like IFN-γ. This leads to excessive inflammation, granuloma formation (appearing as ring-enhancing lesions on MRI), and further neurological damage. This phenomenon is sometimes referred to as Immune Reconstitution Inflammatory Syndrome (IRIS) in the context of HIV, although the exact mechanism here is more related to a dysregulated T cell response to the initial infection/damage. The key is the *aberrant activation of CD4+ T helper cells* leading to excessive inflammation.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A) Formation of immune complexes leading to vasculitis in the brain parenchyma. While immune complexes can cause vasculitis (e.g., in lupus), the CSF profile (lymphocytic pleocytosis with CD4+ predominance and high IFN-γ) is not typical for immune complex-mediated vasculitis. Vasculitis usually involves IgG/IgM deposition and complement activation, not primarily a Th1 response.
- (B) Direct cytopathic effect of the pathogen on neurons and glial cells. While *N. meningitidis* can cause neuronal damage, the worsening symptoms *after* antibiotic initiation and the specific CSF profile (high CD4+ T cells, IFN-γ) strongly suggest an immune-mediated process rather than ongoing direct pathogen damage. The negative cultures also argue against active, high-level infection.
- (C) Activation of CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes targeting infected brain cells. CD8+ T cells are primarily involved in killing infected cells. While they might play a role, the CSF shows a *predominance of CD4+ T cells*, not CD8+ T cells. The high IFN-γ is characteristic of Th1 responses driven by CD4+ cells, which activate macrophages and other immune cells, rather than direct CTL killing.
- (D) Defective phagocytosis of the pathogen by microglia due to complement deficiency. While complement deficiency can impair phagocytosis, the patient's presentation is more consistent with an *overactive* immune response (excessive inflammation, high IFN-γ) rather than a defect in phagocytosis. Furthermore, the initial neutrophilic response suggests phagocytosis was initially occurring. The key finding is the *lymphocytic* shift dominated by CD4+ cells.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | Correct Answer (E) - Aberrant CD4+ T cell activation | (A) Immune Complex Vasculitis | (B) Direct Pathogen Effect | (C) CD8+ T cell Activation | (D) Phagocytosis Defect |
|-------------------------|------------------------------------------------------|-----------------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------|------------------------|
| **Primary Cell Type**   | CD4+ T helper cells                                  | B cells, Neutrophils        | Neurons, Glial cells      | CD8+ Cytotoxic T cells    | Microglia, Neutrophils |
| **Key Cytokine**        | IFN-γ                                                | N/A                         | N/A                       | IFN-γ, TNF-α              | N/A                    |
| **CSF Profile**         | Lymphocytic, CD4+ predominance, High IFN-γ           | Variable, often neutrophilic | Variable                  | Lymphocytic, CD8+ possible | Variable               |
| **MRI Findings**        | Ring-enhancing lesions (granulomas/abscesses)        | Variable                    | Diffuse edema/infarcts    | Variable                  | Variable               |
| **Timing**              | Occurs *after* initial infection/treatment           | Can occur anytime           | During active infection   | During active infection   | Congenital/Early life  |
| **Underlying Condition**| HIV (immunodeficiency/dysregulation)                 | Autoimmune diseases        | Active infection          | Active infection          | Complement deficiency  |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
The key takeaway is recognizing that persistent or worsening neurological symptoms after initial treatment for meningitis, coupled with a shift to a lymphocytic CSF profile dominated by CD4+ T cells and high IFN-γ, points towards an aberrant, excessive cell-mediated immune response (Th1-driven inflammation) rather than ongoing active infection or direct pathogen damage. This is particularly relevant in immunocompromised patients like those with untreated HIV. Differential diagnoses include IRIS, paradoxical reaction to treatment, or other opportunistic infections triggering a strong immune response.

<a id='question-132'></a>

### Question 132: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 3 Focus 32 (recognition)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of MHC class I molecules in presenting endogenous antigens to CD8+ T cells, and how defects in this pathway (e.g., TAP deficiency, TAPBP deficiency, β2-microglobulin deficiency, MHC class I deficiency) lead to impaired CD8+ T cell activation and increased susceptibility to intracellular pathogens, particularly viral infections and certain bacterial infections like Listeria monocytogenes. (理解 MHC I 类分子在呈递内源性抗原给 CD8+ T 细胞中的作用，以及该通路缺陷（例如 TAP 缺乏、TAPBP 缺乏、β2-微球蛋白缺乏、MHC I 类分子缺乏）如何导致 CD8+ T 细胞激活受损并增加对内源性病原体（特别是病毒和某些细菌，如单核细胞增生李斯特菌）的易感性。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old previously healthy woman presents with a 6-month history of recurrent, severe bacterial infections, including pneumonia, sinusitis, and skin abscesses caused by *Staphylococcus aureus* and *Listeria monocytogenes*. She also reports experiencing multiple episodes of severe influenza-like illness. Her medical history is unremarkable except for a childhood history of severe varicella infection. Physical examination reveals no significant abnormalities. Laboratory studies show normal levels of immunoglobulins (IgG, IgA, IgM) and normal complement levels (C3, C4, CH50). Flow cytometry analysis of peripheral blood lymphocytes reveals a normal number of CD4+ T cells but a significantly reduced number of CD8+ T cells (approximately 10% of total lymphocytes, normal range 25-45%). Further investigation reveals that her cells express normal levels of MHC class I molecules on their surface, but intracellular staining shows a marked reduction in the levels of β2-microglobulin (β2m) within the CD8+ T cells.

(A) Defective processing of exogenous antigens presented by MHC class II molecules to CD4+ T cells.
(B) Impaired intracellular trafficking of MHC class I molecules from the endoplasmic reticulum to the cell surface.
(C) Defective transport of peptides from the cytosol into the endoplasmic reticulum for loading onto MHC class I molecules.
(D) Failure of CD8+ T cells to recognize antigens presented by MHC class I molecules due to a mutation in the T cell receptor (TCR) signaling pathway.
(E) Defective interaction between CD8+ T cells and antigen-presenting cells (APCs) due to a deficiency in adhesion molecules like LFA-1.

#### Correct Answer: (C)

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Recurrent severe bacterial infections (S. aureus, L. monocytogenes):** Suggests a defect in cell-mediated immunity, as these intracellular pathogens are primarily controlled by T cells (especially CD8+ for Listeria) and phagocytes activated by T cells.
- **Recurrent severe viral infections (influenza-like illness):** Strongly points towards a defect in CD8+ T cell function, which is crucial for clearing intracellular viruses.
- **Normal immunoglobulins and complement:** Rules out humoral immunodeficiency (B cell defects) and complement deficiencies as the primary cause.
- **Reduced CD8+ T cell number:** Indicates a problem specifically affecting CD8+ T cells or their development/survival.
- **Normal MHC class I expression on cell surface:** Rules out defects in the overall synthesis or transport of MHC class I molecules *to the cell surface*.
- **Reduced intracellular β2-microglobulin (β2m) levels in CD8+ T cells:** This is the key finding. β2m is essential for the proper folding, stability, and cell surface expression of MHC class I heavy chains. Reduced β2m leads to unstable MHC class I molecules that are rapidly degraded, resulting in impaired presentation of endogenous antigens (like viral peptides or bacterial peptides from Listeria) to CD8+ T cells.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
- **MHC Class I Antigen Presentation:** MHC class I molecules are expressed on almost all nucleated cells. They present peptides derived from *endogenous* proteins (e.g., viral proteins synthesized during infection, or bacterial proteins from intracellular bacteria like Listeria) to CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs).
- **Peptide Loading Pathway:** Endogenous proteins are degraded in the cytosol by the proteasome. The resulting peptides are transported into the endoplasmic reticulum (ER) by the TAP (Transporter associated with Antigen Processing) complex (TAP1 and TAP2). Inside the ER, peptides bind to the peptide-binding groove of MHC class I heavy chains, which are associated with β2-microglobulin (β2m) and tapasin. β2m is crucial for stabilizing the MHC class I heavy chain structure and facilitating its transport to the cell surface.
- **CD8+ T Cell Activation:** CD8+ T cells recognize peptide-MHC class I complexes via their T cell receptor (TCR). Co-stimulation (e.g., CD28-B7 interaction) and cytokine signals (e.g., IL-12) are also required for full activation. Activated CD8+ T cells kill infected cells.
- **β2-Microglobulin Deficiency:** β2m is a non-polymorphic heavy chain associated with MHC class I molecules. It is encoded by a gene on chromosome 15. Deficiency leads to impaired MHC class I surface expression, defective CD8+ T cell activation, and increased susceptibility to intracellular pathogens, particularly viruses and Listeria. The vignette describes a patient with reduced intracellular β2m, consistent with this deficiency. The normal surface MHC I expression is likely due to some residual, unstable MHC I molecules reaching the surface, but the overall presentation capacity is severely compromised.
- **Listeria monocytogenes:** An intracellular bacterium primarily controlled by cell-mediated immunity, specifically CD8+ T cells and activated macrophages. Defects in MHC class I presentation lead to increased susceptibility.
- **Influenza Virus:** An intracellular virus primarily controlled by CD8+ T cells. Defects in MHC class I presentation lead to increased susceptibility.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- (A): Defective processing of *exogenous* antigens presented by MHC class II molecules to CD4+ T cells. This describes defects in antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like macrophages or dendritic cells, leading to impaired CD4+ T cell activation and humoral immunity defects. The patient has normal immunoglobulins and a primary defect affecting CD8+ T cells, making this incorrect.
- (B): Impaired intracellular trafficking of MHC class I molecules from the endoplasmic reticulum to the cell surface. This describes TAP deficiency (TAP1 or TAP2 deficiency) or TAPBP deficiency (deficiency in tapasin, which helps load peptides onto MHC I in the ER). While these defects impair MHC I presentation, the key finding here is reduced *β2-microglobulin*, not a defect in the TAP complex or tapasin. TAP deficiency would also lead to reduced MHC I surface expression, which is not the primary finding described (though it might be reduced).
- (D): Failure of CD8+ T cells to recognize antigens presented by MHC class I molecules due to a mutation in the TCR signaling pathway. This would affect the T cell's ability to respond *after* recognizing the antigen, but the primary defect described is impaired antigen presentation itself (due to β2m deficiency), not a defect in the TCR signaling cascade. Also, the reduced CD8+ T cell number is not typically explained by a TCR signaling defect alone.
- (E): Defective interaction between CD8+ T cells and APCs due to a deficiency in adhesion molecules like LFA-1. This describes Leukocyte Adhesion Deficiency type 1 (LAD-1), which affects all leukocytes, leading to impaired migration to sites of infection and recurrent bacterial infections, but typically does not specifically target CD8+ T cell function or MHC class I presentation in this manner. The patient's specific susceptibility pattern (viruses, Listeria) and the specific finding of reduced β2m point away from LAD-1.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                     | β2-Microglobulin Deficiency (Correct Answer) | TAP Deficiency (Distractor B) | MHC Class II Deficiency (Distractor A) | LAD-1 (Distractor E) |
|-----------------------------|---------------------------------------------|------------------------------|---------------------------------------|----------------------|
| **Primary Defect**          | Reduced β2m levels                          | Defective TAP complex        | Defective MHC Class II expression     | LFA-1 deficiency     |
| **MHC Class I Expression**  | Reduced stability, some surface expression | Severely reduced/absent     | Normal                                | Normal               |
| **MHC Class II Expression** | Normal                                      | Normal                       | Severely reduced/absent               | Normal               |
| **Affected T Cell Subset** | CD8+ T cells primarily affected              | CD8+ T cells primarily affected | CD4+ T cells primarily affected       | All leukocytes       |
| **Susceptibility**          | Viruses, Listeria, severe varicella        | Viruses, Listeria            | Extracellular bacteria, fungi, viruses | Recurrent bacterial infections, impaired wound healing |
| **Key Lab Finding**         | Low intracellular β2m                      | Low MHC I surface expression | Low MHC II surface expression        | Impaired leukocyte adhesion |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Defects in the MHC class I antigen presentation pathway, such as β2-microglobulin deficiency, TAP deficiency, or MHC class I deficiency itself, impair CD8+ T cell activation and lead to increased susceptibility to intracellular pathogens like viruses and Listeria monocytogenes. Differentiating these defects requires careful analysis of clinical presentation and specific laboratory findings (e.g., MHC expression levels, β2m levels, TAP complex function). Remember that β2m is essential for MHC class I stability and surface expression.

<a id='question-133'></a>

### Question 133: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 3 Focus 33 (mediated)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of specific cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) subsets (CD8+ T cells) and their mechanisms in eliminating infected or abnormal host cells, particularly in the context of viral infections and graft rejection, and differentiating them from other immune cell types and mechanisms. (理解特异性细胞毒性T淋巴细胞 (CTL) 子集 (CD8+ T细胞) 及其在清除受感染或异常宿主细胞中的机制，特别是在病毒感染和移植物排斥的背景下，并将其与其他免疫细胞类型和机制区分开来。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male, recently diagnosed with HIV infection (CD4 count 150 cells/µL), presents with a 3-week history of persistent, painful oral ulcers and a diffuse vesicular rash on his trunk. He reports no fever or systemic symptoms. Physical examination reveals multiple erythematous papules and vesicles, some coalescing into larger erosions, primarily on the chest and abdomen. A biopsy of a skin lesion shows epidermal necrosis and lymphocytic infiltrate. Viral culture from an oral ulcer grows Cytomegalovirus (CMV). The patient is started on ganciclovir. Despite treatment, the lesions worsen, and he develops signs of disseminated CMV infection, including pneumonitis and colitis. Further investigation reveals that his CMV-specific T-cell response, assessed by interferon-gamma ELISPOT assay, is significantly diminished compared to healthy controls. Which of the following cell types is MOST likely deficient in this patient, leading to the uncontrolled CMV replication and disease progression?

(A) CD4+ T helper cells
(B) Natural Killer (NK) cells
(C) CD8+ cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs)
(D) B lymphocytes producing CMV-specific neutralizing antibodies
(E) Macrophages phagocytosing infected cells

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male with HIV (CD4 count 150 cells/µL), indicating severe immunosuppression.
*   **Clinical Presentation:** Persistent oral ulcers and diffuse vesicular rash (suggestive of herpesvirus infection, specifically CMV given the culture result).
*   **Diagnosis:** Confirmed CMV infection (oral ulcer culture positive).
*   **Disease Course:** Worsening disseminated CMV disease (pneumonitis, colitis) despite antiviral therapy (ganciclovir). This suggests a failure of the immune system to control the virus, not just a lack of drug efficacy.
*   **Immunological Finding:** Diminished CMV-specific T-cell response (IFN-γ ELISPOT). This points towards a defect in cell-mediated immunity against CMV.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely deficient cell type responsible for the uncontrolled CMV replication.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **CMV Pathogenesis:** CMV is a herpesvirus that establishes latent infection. Reactivation or primary infection can cause severe disease, especially in immunocompromised individuals.
*   **Immune Control of CMV:** Control of CMV infection relies heavily on cell-mediated immunity, specifically cytotoxic T lymphocytes (CTLs). CTLs recognize viral peptides presented on MHC class I molecules on the surface of infected cells and kill these cells, preventing viral spread. CD4+ T helper cells are also crucial, providing help for CTL activation and antibody production, but the primary effector cells for clearing infected cells are CTLs.
*   **HIV and Cell-Mediated Immunity:** HIV primarily infects and destroys CD4+ T helper cells, leading to profound immunodeficiency. While CD4+ cells are affected, the direct control of intracellular pathogens like CMV relies on CTLs (CD8+ T cells). In advanced HIV, CTL function can also be impaired, but the primary defect is CD4+ cell depletion. However, the question specifically points to a diminished *CMV-specific* T-cell response, which is primarily mediated by CTLs.
*   **CTL Function:** CD8+ CTLs recognize viral antigens presented by MHC class I molecules on infected cells. Upon recognition, they release cytotoxic granules (perforin, granzymes) or induce apoptosis via Fas/FasL interaction, killing the infected cell. IFN-γ production by CTLs is a key indicator of their activation and function.
*   **Role of Other Cells:**
    *   CD4+ T helper cells: Provide help for CTL activation and B cell antibody production. Their depletion in HIV contributes to overall immune dysfunction but isn't the primary effector cell against CMV-infected cells.
    *   NK cells: Part of the innate immune system, can kill infected cells lacking MHC class I expression (immune evasion strategy by viruses). They play a role early in infection but are less specific than CTLs.
    *   B lymphocytes: Produce antibodies, which can neutralize extracellular virus but are less effective against intracellular viruses like CMV residing within cells.
    *   Macrophages: Phagocytose infected cells and debris, present antigens, but are not the primary killers of CMV-infected cells.

*   **Conclusion:** The patient's severe, uncontrolled CMV disease despite antiviral therapy, coupled with a diminished CMV-specific T-cell response (measured by IFN-γ ELISPOT, a marker of CTL activation), strongly indicates a deficiency in CD8+ CTL function. This is the critical arm of the immune system responsible for eliminating CMV-infected cells.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
*   **(A) CD4+ T helper cells:** While HIV severely depletes CD4+ cells, and this contributes to the overall immunodeficiency, the *specific* failure to control CMV replication (an intracellular virus) points more directly to the effector cells (CTLs) responsible for killing infected cells. CD4+ cells are important for *helping* CTL responses, but the CTLs themselves are the primary killers. The diminished CMV-specific T-cell response measured by IFN-γ ELISPOT is primarily attributed to CTLs.
*   **(B) Natural Killer (NK) cells:** NK cells are part of the innate immune response and can kill virus-infected cells, especially those downregulating MHC class I. However, adaptive immunity, specifically CTLs, is crucial for controlling established CMV infections. While NK cells might contribute, the primary defect in controlling CMV in this context is likely the adaptive CTL response.
*   **(D) B lymphocytes producing CMV-specific neutralizing antibodies:** Antibodies are generally less effective against intracellular viruses like CMV. While antibodies might play a minor role in preventing spread, the main mechanism for clearing CMV-infected cells is CTL-mediated cytotoxicity. The diminished T-cell response specifically points away from a primary B-cell defect.
*   **(E) Macrophages phagocytosing infected cells:** Macrophages play a role in antigen presentation and clearing cellular debris, but they are not the primary effector cells responsible for killing CMV-infected cells. CTLs are the specialized cells for this task.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | CD8+ CTLs (Correct Answer)                                  | CD4+ T Helper Cells (Distractor A)                               | NK Cells (Distractor B)                                     | B Lymphocytes (Distractor D)                               | Macrophages (Distractor E)                                  |
| :---------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------- | :----------------------------------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------------- |
| **Primary Role in CMV** | Kill CMV-infected cells (adaptive, specific)                 | Help activate CTLs & B cells (adaptive, non-specific killing)    | Kill infected cells (innate, less specific)                  | Produce antibodies (humoral, less effective against CMV)     | Phagocytosis, antigen presentation (innate/adaptive, non-specific killing) |
| **MHC Restriction**     | MHC Class I                                                 | MHC Class II (for antigen presentation)                          | Not MHC restricted (recognize stress ligands, lack MHC I)   | Not directly involved in cell killing via MHC                | Not directly involved in cell killing via MHC                |
| **Key Cytokine**        | IFN-γ, TNF-α, Granzymes, Perforin                            | IL-2, IFN-γ, IL-4, IL-5, IL-13 (depending on subset)             | IFN-γ, TNF-α                                                 | Primarily antibody secretion                                  | Cytokines for inflammation & antigen presentation           |
| **Deficiency Impact**   | Uncontrolled intracellular viral replication, graft rejection | Impaired adaptive immunity, increased susceptibility to infections | Impaired early control of viral infections, tumor surveillance | Impaired humoral immunity, extracellular bacterial infections | Impaired phagocytosis, chronic inflammation, granuloma formation |
| **HIV Impact**          | Can be impaired, but primary target is CD4+ cells           | Primary target of HIV, leading to severe immunodeficiency        | Generally less affected by HIV                               | Can be affected indirectly                                   | Can be affected indirectly                                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
Cell-mediated immunity, particularly CD8+ CTLs, is critical for controlling intracellular pathogens like CMV. A diminished CMV-specific T-cell response (often measured by IFN-γ ELISPOT or proliferation assays) in an immunocompromised patient points towards a defect in CTL function. Differentiate CTL function from CD4+ T helper cell function (providing help), NK cell function (innate killing), B cell function (antibody production), and macrophage function (phagocytosis/antigen presentation). Remember that HIV primarily targets CD4+ cells, but the control of intracellular pathogens relies heavily on CTLs.

<a id='question-134'></a>

### Question 134: Cell-Mediated Immunity: Part 3 Focus 34 (principally)
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the role of different T cell subsets (specifically Th1 vs. Th2) in orchestrating immune responses against intracellular pathogens and extracellular parasites, and how dysregulation can lead to specific clinical manifestations. (理解不同T细胞亚群（特别是Th1 vs. Th2）在对抗细胞内病原体和胞外寄生虫中的作用，以及失调如何导致特定临床表现。)

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old male presents to the clinic with a 3-week history of persistent cough, low-grade fever, and night sweats. He is a recent immigrant from a region endemic for tuberculosis. His chest X-ray reveals bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy and diffuse interstitial infiltrates. Sputum smears are positive for acid-fast bacilli (AFB). He is started on standard anti-tuberculosis therapy (isoniazid, rifampin, pyrazinamide, ethambutol). However, after 6 months, his symptoms worsen, and new cavitary lesions develop in the upper lobes of both lungs. Further investigation reveals that he is HIV-positive with a CD4+ T cell count of 85 cells/µL. He is also found to have a markedly elevated serum IgE level and eosinophilia. Which of the following immunological mechanisms BEST explains the patient's worsening tuberculosis despite anti-tuberculosis therapy?

(A) Impaired macrophage activation due to deficient IFN-γ production by CD4+ Th1 cells.
(B) Increased susceptibility to opportunistic fungal infections due to deficient IL-17 production by Th17 cells.
(C) Enhanced antibody production against *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* antigens due to increased IL-4 production by CD4+ Th2 cells.
(D) Defective cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) activity due to impaired CD8+ T cell priming by dendritic cells.
(E) Reduced complement activation leading to decreased opsonization of *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* due to deficient C3 levels.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Clinical/Microbiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
*   **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old male, immigrant from TB-endemic region, HIV-positive (CD4 < 200), low CD4 count (85 cells/µL). This indicates severe immunosuppression, particularly affecting cell-mediated immunity.
*   **Initial Presentation:** Symptoms consistent with primary tuberculosis (cough, fever, night sweats, hilar lymphadenopathy, interstitial infiltrates, AFB+ sputum).
*   **Treatment Failure:** Worsening symptoms and development of cavitary lesions despite standard anti-TB therapy. This suggests a failure of the immune system to control the infection, not necessarily drug resistance (though it's possible, the question focuses on the immune mechanism).
*   **Immunological Findings:** Elevated IgE and eosinophilia. These are characteristic features often associated with Th2-skewed immune responses.
*   **Question Stem:** Asks for the immunological mechanism explaining the worsening TB.

##### 2. Immunological, Pathological & Virulence Deep-Dive (免疫机制、微生物致病机理与分子生物学深度剖析)
*   **Tuberculosis Pathogenesis:** *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* is an intracellular pathogen primarily controlled by cell-mediated immunity. Macrophages phagocytose the bacilli, but *M. tuberculosis* prevents phagosome-lysosome fusion. Effective control requires activation of macrophages by IFN-γ produced by CD4+ Th1 cells. Th1 cells are primed by antigen-presenting cells (APCs) like dendritic cells and macrophages presenting mycobacterial antigens via MHC class II to CD4+ T cells in the presence of IL-12. IL-12 drives the differentiation of naive CD4+ T cells into Th1 cells, which then produce IFN-γ. IFN-γ activates macrophages, enhancing their ability to kill intracellular *M. tuberculosis* and form granulomas. CD8+ CTLs also contribute by killing infected cells.
*   **HIV and TB:** HIV infection, particularly with low CD4 counts, severely impairs cell-mediated immunity. CD4+ T cells are the primary target of HIV, leading to a decline in Th1 cell numbers and function. This compromises IFN-γ production, macrophage activation, and granuloma formation, making individuals highly susceptible to TB reactivation or progression.
*   **Th1 vs. Th2 Responses:** Th1 responses (driven by IFN-γ, IL-12) are crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens like *M. tuberculosis*. Th2 responses (driven by IL-4, IL-5, IL-13) are typically involved in responses against extracellular parasites (helminths) and allergic reactions. IL-4 promotes B cell class switching to IgE and eosinophil activation.
*   **HIV and Th2 Skewing:** HIV infection can lead to a skewing of the immune response towards Th2, partly due to chronic antigenic stimulation and potentially direct effects of viral proteins. This Th2 skewing further impairs the ability to control intracellular pathogens like *M. tuberculosis*. The elevated IgE and eosinophilia in this patient support a Th2-dominant environment.
*   **Mechanism in this Case:** The patient's severe HIV infection (low CD4 count) leads to a profound deficiency in CD4+ Th1 cells. This results in insufficient IFN-γ production, leading to impaired macrophage activation. Macrophages cannot effectively kill the intracellular *M. tuberculosis*, leading to progressive disease and cavitation despite antibiotic treatment. The Th2 skewing (indicated by high IgE/eosinophilia) further exacerbates the Th1 deficiency.

##### 3. Option Analysis
*   **(A) Impaired macrophage activation due to deficient IFN-γ production by CD4+ Th1 cells.** This aligns perfectly with the pathophysiology. HIV depletes CD4+ T cells, including Th1 cells. Lack of Th1 cells means less IFN-γ. Lack of IFN-γ means macrophages cannot be activated to kill *M. tuberculosis*. This explains the treatment failure and disease progression.
*   **(B) Increased susceptibility to opportunistic fungal infections due to deficient IL-17 production by Th17 cells.** While HIV can affect Th17 cells, the primary problem in TB control is Th1 deficiency. IL-17 is more important for mucosal immunity against extracellular fungi and bacteria. This doesn't directly explain the worsening TB.
*   **(C) Enhanced antibody production against *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* antigens due to increased IL-4 production by CD4+ Th2 cells.** Antibodies are generally ineffective against intracellular bacteria like *M. tuberculosis*. While IL-4 production might be increased (Th2 skewing), this would *hinder* rather than enhance TB control by suppressing Th1 responses.
*   **(D) Defective cytotoxic T lymphocyte (CTL) activity due to impaired CD8+ T cell priming by dendritic cells.** While CD8+ T cells play a role, the primary defect in controlling *M. tuberculosis* in this context is the failure of macrophage activation mediated by CD4+ Th1 cells and IFN-γ. CD4+ help is crucial for optimal CD8+ T cell responses, but the lack of IFN-γ is the more direct and critical failure point here.
*   **(E) Reduced complement activation leading to decreased opsonization of *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* due to deficient C3 levels.** Complement plays a minor role in controlling *M. tuberculosis*, which primarily resides intracellularly. C3 deficiency is not typically associated with HIV or TB progression in this manner.

#### Distractor Analysis (全干扰项逐一深度纠错剖析):
- **(A):** This is the correct answer. The core issue is the lack of IFN-γ from Th1 cells due to HIV-induced CD4+ T cell depletion, leading to ineffective macrophage activation and uncontrolled *M. tuberculosis* growth.
- **(B):** While HIV can impact Th17 cells, the primary immune defect relevant to *M. tuberculosis* control is the Th1 deficiency. IL-17 is more associated with defense against extracellular pathogens and mucosal immunity. Opportunistic fungal infections are common in HIV, but the mechanism described (deficient IL-17) is not the primary reason for worsening TB.
- **(C):** This describes a Th2 response. While HIV can induce Th2 skewing (supported by the high IgE/eosinophilia), a Th2 response is detrimental to controlling intracellular pathogens like *M. tuberculosis*. Increased antibody production (especially IgE) is not helpful and may even worsen the situation by suppressing Th1 responses.
- **(D):** CD8+ T cells (CTLs) are important for killing infected cells, but the primary mechanism for controlling *M. tuberculosis* within macrophages relies on IFN-γ-mediated macrophage activation by CD4+ Th1 cells. While CD4+ help is needed for optimal CTL function, the lack of IFN-γ is the more central defect in this scenario.
- **(E):** Complement is not a major player in controlling intracellular bacteria like *M. tuberculosis*. The bacteria reside within macrophages, largely protected from complement-mediated lysis or opsonization. C3 deficiency is not a characteristic feature of HIV or TB progression.

#### High-Yield Comparison Table (核心考点与易混淆病原体/免疫缺陷横向对比):
| Feature                 | *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* Control | HIV Infection Effect                                  | Correct Mechanism (Option A)                                  |
| :---------------------- | :----------------------------------- | :---------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Key Immune Cell         | CD4+ Th1 cells                        | Depletion of CD4+ T cells (including Th1)             | Deficient IFN-γ production by CD4+ Th1 cells                  |
| Key Cytokine            | IFN-γ                                | Reduced IFN-γ production                               | Impaired macrophage activation                                |
| Target Cell             | Macrophages                          | CD4+ T cells                                          | Macrophage activation failure                                 |
| Primary Immune Response | Cell-mediated (Th1)                   | Skewing towards Th2, overall suppression of CMI       | Impaired macrophage killing of intracellular bacteria         |
| Clinical Manifestation  | Granuloma formation, control of infection | Increased susceptibility to intracellular pathogens, opportunistic infections | Worsening TB despite therapy, cavitation                   |

#### Educational Objective (USMLE High-Yield Takeaway & Differentials):
HIV infection severely compromises cell-mediated immunity, particularly Th1 responses crucial for controlling intracellular pathogens like *Mycobacterium tuberculosis*. The lack of IFN-γ production by CD4+ Th1 cells leads to impaired macrophage activation, resulting in progressive TB disease despite treatment. Differentiate this from Th2-mediated responses (allergies, helminth infections), Th17-mediated responses (mucosal immunity), or humoral immunity defects (antibody deficiencies).
